Tailored interventions to address determinants of practice
Authors: Sheena McHugh, Fiona Riordan, Aoife O'Mahony, Laura-Jane McCarthy, Ana Contreras Navarro, Claire Kerins, Jane Murphy, Eimear C Morrissey, Eilis J O'Reilly, Siobhan O'Connor, Danielle R Adams, Rosemary Meza, Cara C Lewis, Byron J. Powell, Michel Wensing, Signe A. Flottorp, Luke Wolfenden
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
AbstractRationaleTailored implementation strategies are frequently recommended to improve healthcare professional practice. Tailoring involves the selection and design of strategies to address context‐specific barriers (referred to as determinants of practice) to best practice recommended in clinical guidelines. Improvements in practice are thought to be more likely if implementation strategies are selected to address identified practice determinants. This is an update of a review published in 2015.ObjectivesTo assess the effect of tailored implementation strategies, compared with a non‐tailored strategy or no strategy, in improving healthcare professional practice.Secondary objectives were to assess whether the effects of tailored implementation strategies differ according to whether theory, evidence of the effectiveness of strategies, and input from stakeholders were involved in the tailoring process, and to assess whether the effects of tailored implementation strategies differ according to setting (high‐ or low‐income country).Search methodsWe searched CENTRAL, MEDLINE, Embase, two other databases and two trials registers from 2014 to 5 March 2024. We performed a forward citation search for papers citing the previous update. We did not apply any restrictions on date of publication, publication status or language.Eligibility criteriaWe included randomised controlled trials (RCTs), including cluster‐RCTs, that compared tailored implementation strategies with strategies not tailored to address determinants of health professional practice, or no strategy. We excluded studies of tailored strategies targeting behaviour change among patients only.OutcomesThe primary outcome was healthcare professional practice assessed using measures of adherence to recommended practices or guidelines in a healthcare setting.Risk of biasWe used the Cochrane risk of bias tool version 2 (RoB 2) to assess risk of bias in the studies.Synthesis methodsReview authors (working in pairs) screened all citations, extracted data, and assessed risk of bias independently and in duplicate. A third review author resolved disagreements. We performed meta‐analyses using random‐effects models for the primary outcome using the most conservative estimate of effect where multiple outcomes were reported. We also performed meta‐analyses using the least conservative estimate in sensitivity analysis. Where data were unsuitable for pooling in meta‐analyses, we conducted a narrative synthesis using a vote‐counting approach. We assessed heterogeneity using the I² statistic and the certainty of the evidence for the main comparison using GRADE.Included studiesThe previous version of this review included 32 studies. In this update, we excluded five of those studies as they no longer met the review’s eligibility criteria; they did not meet the definition of tailoring or focused on patient outcomes only. We added 52 new studies (including two from trials registers), bringing the total number of included studies to 79. Participant numbers were available for 41 of these studies (n = 25,630); participant counts were not reported or were unclear in the other 38 studies.Synthesis of resultsOverall, 35 studies with a total of 5015 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored implementation strategy on professional practice compared to a non‐tailored strategy. In total, 44 studies with a total of 20,615 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored strategy on professional practice compared to no strategy.We judged 41 of the 45 studies included in the meta‐analysis as free from high risks of bias across all domains. Bias in the measurement of the outcome was the most common domain judged at high risk of bias among the remaining studies. Overall, the certainty of the evidence in the main comparison (tailored vs non‐tailored) was moderate, reflecting visual inconsistency and high heterogeneity between study results.We found that tailored implementation strategies probably lead to a slight improvement in professional practice compared with non‐tailored strategies (odds ratio (OR) 1.49, 95% confidence interval (CI) 1.26 to 1.75; 23 studies, 2600 participants, moderate‐certainty evidence). An OR greater than 1 indicates a small improvement in professional practice compared with no strategy. Some studies included effect measures for more than one outcome. Sensitivity analysis pooling the least conservative effect measures that were reported showed an OR of 1.74 (95% CI 1.3 to 2.24.For tailored implementation strategies compared with no strategy, we report a pooled OR of 1.34 (95% CI 1.17 to 1.53; 24 studies, 8250 participants). Some studies included effect measures for more than one outcome. Sensitivity analysis pooling the least conservative effect measures that were reported showed an OR of 1.51 (95% CI 1.28 to 1.79; P < 0.0001).Authors' conclusionsDespite the increase in the number of new studies identified, our overall finding is like that of the previous review. Tailored implementation strategies probably result in a slight improvement in professional practice.FundingDr Sheena McHugh received funding from the Irish Health Research Board (HRB‐RL‐2020‐004) that helped to support the conduct of this review.RegistrationRegistration: Zenodo, 7104299, via DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.7104299Protocol available via DOI: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.7104299Previous versions available via: DOI: 10.1002/14651858.CD005470.pub3, DOI: 10.1002/14651858.CD005470.pub2, and DOI: 10.1002/14651858.CD005470

Moderate certainty: we are moderately confident in the effect estimate: the true effect is likely to be close to the estimate of the effect, but there is a possibility that it is substantially different. Low certainty: our confidence in the effect estimate is limited: the true effect may be substantially different from the estimate of the effect. Very low certainty: we have very little confidence in the effect estimate: the true effect is likely to be substantially different from the estimate of effect.aFor this comparison, the outcome ‘implementation of recommended practice’ was measured in all included studies as adherence to recommendations in clinical guidelines. Outcomes were broadly categorised as preventative screening or assessment, management or treatment, prescribing or medication initiation, recording, referral or composite measures of guideline adherence. One outcome within this comparison was classified as ‘other’, relating to bed occupancy rates. Although the specific practices varied by study, all measures reflected implementation of guideline‑recommended care. bThe basis for the assumed risk (e.g. the median control group risk across studies) is based on those reported by Baker 2015 [1]. According to their review, the assumed risks without a tailored intervention were selected to help interpret the overall odds ratios in situations in which there is a high risk of undesirable professional practice without intervening (20% desired practice) and a medium risk (60% desired practice). The corresponding risk (and its 95% confidence interval) is based on the assumed risk in the comparison group and the relative effect of the intervention (and its 95% CI). c'Moderate' and 'low' adherence refer to different assumed baseline risks in the control group, with ‘moderate’ representing a typical (median) control group adherence rate and ‘low’ representing a lower adherence scenario. The same relative effect (OR 1.49, 95% CI 1.26 to 1.75) is applied to each baseline risk to calculate absolute effects. dSubstantially different = a large enough difference that it might affect a decision. eThe OR and confidence intervals shown are taken from a meta‐analysis. The results of 13 studies not included in the meta‐analysis indicated that, on average, tailored interventions improve professional practice. However, the effects were mixed. fRisk of bias: we identified some concerns for all domains across varying numbers of studies, most frequently related to deviations from intended interventions. However, we did not judge any study or domain to be at high risk of bias. We therefore did not downgrade for risk of bias. gInconsistency: we downgraded by one level for inconsistency due to substantial heterogeneity, as shown by both visual inconsistency in forest plots and statistical measures of heterogeneity (I² = 82%). hImprecision: we did not downgrade for imprecision. Although some individual studies had small sample sizes and wide confidence intervals, the pooled effect estimate was based on a large total sample size and had a narrow confidence interval. Because GRADE assesses imprecision using the pooled estimate rather than individual studies, and the slightly wide CI for the risk ratio is more likely due to heterogeneity, we considered the evidence to be sufficiently precise. iIndirectness: we did not downgrade for indirectness. The populations, interventions, comparisons, and outcomes assessed in the included studies were considered sufficiently similar to those of interest in this review, and the evidence was judged to answer the review question directly. jPublication bias: we did not downgrade for publication bias because the search strategy was comprehensive (including trials registries and protocol checks), and although the funnel plot showed some slight asymmetry, this was not considered substantial enough to indicate publication bias.

Background

Description of the condition

In healthcare, gaps frequently arise between what is recommended by evidence‐based clinical guidelines and supported by research, and what is implemented in practice [2]. Although there are growing numbers of evidence‐based interventions to prevent and treat illness, and to co‐ordinate care for people, these interventions may not be adopted widely or implemented as intended. Much of the research in the fields of health services and implementation science has sought to understand the reasons for these gaps and find ways to address them. For example, a recent umbrella review of systematic reviews identified 193 barriers to and 140 facilitators of clinical practice guideline implementation [3]. Effective implementation strategies are needed to address the barriers to change.

Implementation strategies are “methods or techniques used to enhance the adoption, implementation, and sustainment of a clinical program or practice” [4], and are distinct from evidence‐based interventions themselves. There are several published taxonomies describing implementation strategies [5, 6, 7, 8]. Strategies are typically multi‐faceted and involve different processes, for example, planning (conducting a needs assessment), educating (conducting ongoing training), financing (altering incentives), and managing quality (clinician reminders, audit and feedback) [8]. The determinants of professional practice are also multi‐level, and may include individual (lack of knowledge), organisational (competing priorities), and broader system (limited resourcing or financing) factors. As such, the design of effective implementation strategies can be challenging. Tailoring is frequently suggested as an important consideration when selecting and modifying strategies to address determinants of implementation [9].

Description of the intervention and how it might work

Tailoring

This review is an update of a Cochrane review examining the effects of tailored implementation strategies on recommended professional practice (as set out in clinical practice guidelines). It was first published in 2005 [10], and subsequently updated in 2010 [11], and 2015 [12]. The current review uses the terminology of tailored implementation strategies, recognising that an 'implementation strategy' is typically a complex intervention comprising a bundle or set of multiple components. While 'implementation intervention' is sometimes used interchangeably with 'implementation strategy', we use the latter term here to avoid confusion with evidence‐based interventions (the focus of implementation) [13], and given the use of this term within the field of implementation science [14]. Implementation strategies may be delivered by different types of actors, such as implementation facilitators or 'coaches' or fellow clinical staff [15]. Strategies typically target those responsible for implementation (e.g. healthcare professionals) or those making decisions about implementation (e.g. managers and administrators).

Definitions of the term ‘tailoring’ vary considerably, with differences in how the term is conceptualised, operationalised, and applied [16]. However, tailoring is generally described as a prospective process for selecting and modifying strategies to address contextual determinants of practice [12, 17]. Determinants refer to any factor influencing implementation in a particular context and, subsequently, intervention effectiveness. These factors are sometimes referred to as barriers or obstacles to implementation, and enablers or facilitators of implementation. Several frameworks have been developed to categorise determinants including the Tailored Implementation in Chronic Disease (TICD) checklist used in the previous review [18], Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) [19], and the Exploration, Preparation, Implementation, Sustainment (EPIS) framework [20].

Consistent with the definitions used in the previous version of this review, in this update we define tailored implementation strategies as planned activities selected and designed to enhance professional practice by addressing prospectively identified determinants of that practice. We continue to focus on the implementation of one type of evidence‐based intervention, clinical practice guideline‐based management, using health professional adherence to guidelines as a measure of effect. It is important to note that while we define and focus on tailoring as a prospective design process as per the previous review, it can also be considered an ongoing process that continues after the initial implementation strategy has been deployed to iteratively adapt strategies as context changes [21]. Furthermore, tailoring itself may be part of the implementation strategy [22, 23, 24]. For example, an implementation strategy could involve facilitation to support or coach staff to tailor their own implementation strategies to meet site‐specific needs [25].

The tailoring process assumes that systematically selecting the implementation strategies to address identified determinants of practice is more likely to lead to implementation success and thus evidence‐based intervention effectiveness. This contrasts with selecting and applying a universal implementation strategy based on previous experience alone, or assumptions about the determinants of practice for a particular evidence‐based intervention in a given setting. Mismatches between implementation determinants and strategies may impede the effectiveness of efforts to improve professional practice [26, 27]. There are now several expert‐ and theory‐informed approaches to guide the matching of determinants and strategies, such as the CFIR‐Expert Recommendations on Implementing Change (ERIC) matching tool [28], Implementation Mapping [29], a tool for linking behaviour‐change techniques and mechanisms of action [30], and Causal Pathway Diagramming [31], which are recommended to support better matching of implementation determinant to strategy. The TICD checklist includes five worksheets to facilitate its use, supporting the identification and prioritisation of determinants of practice and the development of implementation strategies [18].

Why it is important to do this review

We have not identified any reviews that evaluated the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies to improve professional practice other than the earlier versions of this review. The most recent version published in 2015 identified 32 studies and reported a small to moderate effect of tailored implementation strategies compared to either no strategy or a non‐tailored strategy [12]. The authors concluded that methods for tailoring were not well‐developed or thoroughly described in published studies and there was limited evidence on the applicability of methods to low‐income countries and disadvantaged groups. Hence, we believe it is important to update this review to provide a comprehensive account of the available evidence.

Since the publication of the first Cochrane review on tailored interventions to overcome barriers to change 20 years ago, the research field of tailored implementation strategies to improve professional practice has progressed substantially. Tailoring has become a popular practice. There are now more opportunities to publish studies dedicated to evaluations of implementation strategies with the increasing popularity and impact of the flagship journal Implementation Science and the establishment of other dedicated journals such as Implementation Science Communications (2020), Implementation Research and Practice (2020), and Global Implementation Research and Applications (2021). Recent studies suggest that tailored implementation strategies positively affect professional practice [32].

To inform and improve the implementation of evidence‐based interventions, we sought to provide a comprehensive and updated synthesis of randomised controlled trials (RCTs) investigating the effect of tailored implementation strategies on professional practice in healthcare settings.

Objectives

Primary objective

To assess the effect of tailored implementation strategies, compared with a non‐tailored strategy or no strategy, in improving healthcare professional practice.

Secondary objectives

To assess whether the effects of tailored implementation strategies differ according to whether theory, evidence of the effectiveness of strategies, and input from stakeholders were involved in the tailoring process.To assess whether the effects of tailored implementation strategies differ according to setting (high‐ or low‐income country).

To assess whether the effects of tailored implementation strategies differ according to setting (high‐ or low‐income country).

To assess whether the effects of tailored implementation strategies differ according to setting (high‐ or low‐income country).

Methods

Differences between protocol and review

The review was conducted in accordance with the Methodological Expectations of Cochrane Intervention Reviews (MECIR) standards [33], and reporting was guided by the PRISMA 2020 guideline [34].

We made four key changes to the scope of the review.

First, we included all RCT designs, except cross‐over RCTs, given such designs are impractical for health services research and the likelihood that carry‐over effects would distort effect estimates. The previous review focused on cluster‐RCTs only.Second, we included additional terms in the search strategy. In recent years, particular terms have been used when referring to approaches to tailoring (e.g. implementation mapping, intervention mapping) and intervention development (i.e. co‐design, co‐production, co‐creation).Third, we switched the main comparison to tailored versus non‐tailored implementation strategies.Lastly, we focused on professional practice outcomes only. The previous review focused on both professional practice and patient outcomes. We considered that this change was justified given that the primary focus of the review is professional practice and the aim is to understand the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies – as individual professionals (or teams within healthcare organisations) are the target of these interventions, professional‐level measures represent a more proximal outcome of effectiveness.

Third, we switched the main comparison to tailored versus non‐tailored implementation strategies.

Lastly, we focused on professional practice outcomes only. The previous review focused on both professional practice and patient outcomes. We considered that this change was justified given that the primary focus of the review is professional practice and the aim is to understand the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies – as individual professionals (or teams within healthcare organisations) are the target of these interventions, professional‐level measures represent a more proximal outcome of effectiveness.

Lastly, we focused on professional practice outcomes only. The previous review focused on both professional practice and patient outcomes. We considered that this change was justified given that the primary focus of the review is professional practice and the aim is to understand the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies – as individual professionals (or teams within healthcare organisations) are the target of these interventions, professional‐level measures represent a more proximal outcome of effectiveness.

We also made changes to the information extracted from included studies about the constituent implementation strategies being evaluated.

We did not classify the intensity or quality of the methods used to identify determinants and select strategies. This aspect was not critical to achieve the objectives of the review. We plan to conduct a separate study to explore the association between tailoring intensity and strategy effectiveness.We did not record the extent to which the tailored implementation strategies were selected to address local factors. This was investigated by the previous review team as a source of heterogeneity but was not found to be associated with the reported effectiveness of the tailored strategies [12].

A new author team prepared this update, but the team included two authors of the previous version of the review: Signe Flottorp (SF) and Michel Wensing (MW).

Consistent with the previous versions of the review, we continued to focus on the implementation of one type of evidence‐based intervention, clinical practice guideline‐based management. Given the number and variety of evidence‐based interventions implemented across healthcare settings, it was beyond the capacity of the review team to consider all types of evidence‐based interventions.

For differences between the protocol and the review, see Supplementary material 10.

Criteria for considering studies for this review

Types of studies

Eligible studies were RCTs, namely studies where the allocation to the intervention is stated as being randomised, including cluster‐RCTs, except cross‐over trials.

Types of participants

Participants could be individual healthcare professionals or healthcare teams that influence professional practice and the delivery of healthcare (as teams could be targeted by an implementation strategy). Healthcare teams could include healthcare professionals responsible for patient care, administrators, managers, and those responsible for financing or regulating healthcare. To be included, the team had to involve healthcare professionals. We excluded studies that involved only students, trainees or interns.

Types of interventions

Consistent with the previous review, we defined tailored strategies as a collection of “strategies to improve professional practice that are planned, taking account of prospectively identified determinants of practice” [12]. We used the compilation of implementation strategies developed by the ERIC to define and categorise strategies [17]. An implementation strategy could involve a single component or be multi‐faceted, comprising multiple components that potentially influence professional practice. For example, a multifaceted strategy could involve educational materials designed for healthcare professionals, revision of professional roles, along with audit and feedback to support change.

Based on consensus across the review team, we operationalised ‘tailoring’ in a specific way to enable eligibility screening. The studies had to refer to an assessment of determinants of practice in advance of selecting a strategy (i.e. prospective tailoring). We excluded studies of preselected strategies, the delivery of which was subsequently tailored, and preselected strategies that facilitated ongoing tailoring. Studies had to provide some detail on the approach they used to tailor the strategy, such as describing the methods for identifying determinants, or strategy selection taking those determinants into account, or both. These methods could include quantitative, qualitative, or mixed methods, as well as collaborative activities among staff (i.e. observation/survey/interview/focus groups/interviews), or meetings or consultations to obtain feedback from staff, or both.

We excluded a study if the description of the approach to tailoring was limited. For example, many studies make general references to literature describing determinants of practice in the introduction but do not describe research to understand those determinants in their specific context or do not make explicit the link between determinants of practice in that context and the selected strategy. We excluded studies that:

relied solely on previously published literature (i.e. referring to studies other than those conducted by the study team or study authors) to identify determinants of practice; orrelied solely on input from experts in the field or made general statements about locally adjusting, adapting or tailoring to site needs without elaboration on how this was carried out.

relied solely on previously published literature (i.e. referring to studies other than those conducted by the study team or study authors) to identify determinants of practice; or

relied solely on previously published literature (i.e. referring to studies other than those conducted by the study team or study authors) to identify determinants of practice; or

relied solely on input from experts in the field or made general statements about locally adjusting, adapting or tailoring to site needs without elaboration on how this was carried out.

relied solely on input from experts in the field or made general statements about locally adjusting, adapting or tailoring to site needs without elaboration on how this was carried out.

In some cases, study authors referred to prior studies they had undertaken to identify determinants, which were then explicitly used to inform the selection and design of the implementation intervention. In these cases, we also used the studies preceding the study as a source for data extraction.

We excluded studies that used gap analysis only (i.e. audits identifying a gap between actual and desired performance), and studies of educational interventions designed to improve knowledge only. Determinants could be identified by various methods, including observation, brainstorming, focus group discussions, interviews, surveys of the involved healthcare professionals, or analysis of the organisation or system in which care is provided.

Control group

We included the following control groups:

a comparison group that received a non‐tailored strategy (i.e. an implementation strategy that did not explicitly address identified determinants);a comparison group that received no intervention or usual practice (i.e. that did not receive an implementation strategy).

a comparison group that received a non‐tailored strategy (i.e. an implementation strategy that did not explicitly address identified determinants);

a comparison group that received a non‐tailored strategy (i.e. an implementation strategy that did not explicitly address identified determinants);

a comparison group that received no intervention or usual practice (i.e. that did not receive an implementation strategy).

a comparison group that received no intervention or usual practice (i.e. that did not receive an implementation strategy).

Outcome measures

Critical outcomes

Studies had to measure professional practice, that is, adherence of healthcare professionals to guideline‐recommended practice, in a healthcare setting. This outcome is in the category of ‘quality of care’ outlined in the Cochrane Effective Practice and Organisation of Care Group (EPOC) guidance on outcomes to be reported in EPOC reviews [35]. A core outcome set was unavailable when we conducted this Cochrane review update.

Professional practice may be measured by:

dichotomous process outcomes: the percentage of patients receiving a target process of care (e.g. prescription of a specific medication, documentation of performance of a specific task, referral to a consultant) or whose care followed a guideline recommendation;continuous process outcomes: any continuous measure of how providers delivered care (e.g. duration of antibiotic therapy, time to respond to a critical laboratory value).

dichotomous process outcomes: the percentage of patients receiving a target process of care (e.g. prescription of a specific medication, documentation of performance of a specific task, referral to a consultant) or whose care followed a guideline recommendation;

dichotomous process outcomes: the percentage of patients receiving a target process of care (e.g. prescription of a specific medication, documentation of performance of a specific task, referral to a consultant) or whose care followed a guideline recommendation;

continuous process outcomes: any continuous measure of how providers delivered care (e.g. duration of antibiotic therapy, time to respond to a critical laboratory value).

continuous process outcomes: any continuous measure of how providers delivered care (e.g. duration of antibiotic therapy, time to respond to a critical laboratory value).

Consistent with the previous review, professional practice outcomes could include those collected via medical records, claim forms, case notes, or questionnaires or forms, and those reported by patients, for example, via questionnaire or survey [12].

Important outcomes

We did not examine any secondary outcomes in this review.

Search methods for identification of studies

This review represents the fourth update of a review first published in 2005 [10], and most recently updated in 2015 [12]. The EPOC Information Specialist (Nia Roberts) developed the search strategies in consultation with the review authors. The MEDLINE search was peer‐reviewed by an information specialist (Marit Johansen) at the EPOC Norwegian satellite.

We did not apply language restrictions. We used a methodological search filter to limit retrieval to appropriate study designs: the 'Cochrane Highly Sensitive Search Strategy’ to identify RCTs in MEDLINE: sensitivity‐ and precision‐maximising version to identify RCTs [36].

Search strategies comprised keywords and controlled vocabulary terms. We searched all databases from the date of the last search in the previous version of the review (December 2014) to March 2024. Detailed search strategies executed for this update are provided in Supplementary material 1.

Electronic searches

We searched the following electronic databases up to 5 March 2024:

Cochrane Database of Systematic Reviews (CDSR) and Cochrane Central Register of Controlled Trials (CENTRAL; 2024, Issue 3) in the Cochrane Library (2014 to 5 March 2024);MEDLINE Ovid (2014 to 5 March 2024);Embase Ovid (2014 to 5 March 2024);CINAHL EBSCOHost (2014 to 5 March 2024).

We also:

Data collection and analysis

Selection of studies

We downloaded all titles and abstracts retrieved by electronic searching to Covidence and removed duplicates [37]. For this update, pairs of review authors (from FR, EM, SOC, ACN, AOM, DRA, LJMC, MW, CK, SF, SMH) independently screened titles and abstracts using Covidence. We retrieved full texts of all the studies identified as potentially relevant by either one or both review authors. Pairs of review authors (from FR, JM, EM, LJMC, SOC, ACN, CK, DRA, MW, AOM, SMH) independently reviewed the full text of the remaining studies against our inclusion criteria. We excluded studies that were only available in conference proceedings; in practice, we found abstracts lacked sufficient information to judge whether the implementation intervention was tailored. At this stage of the screening process, we resolved disagreements by discussion, involving a third review author (LW, SMH) if necessary. We recorded reasons for the exclusion of studies, after full‐text review, in Supplementary material 3. Review authors involved in the included studies (CCL, MW, SF, LW) did not make eligibility or selection decisions about their own studies.

We identified studies that we classified as ‘awaiting classification’. Studies that met the eligibility criteria for the review but were still in progress and had no results available were classified and reported as 'ongoing studies' (e.g. records of protocols and incomplete clinical trials at the time of writing the manuscript).

Data extraction and management

Pairs of review authors (from FR, JM, EM, LJM, DRA, CK, IC, JB, SMH, AOM) independently extracted data in duplicate using a piloted data extraction form. We resolved any disagreements in data extraction by discussion and consensus, or by consulting a third review author where required. Data were extracted to a Word form and input onto an Excel spreadsheet [38, 39], which we then entered into Review Manager (RevMan) version 8.9.0 [40]. Review authors CCL, SF and MW are authors on one or more of the included studies. They were not involved in the data extraction for these or other studies.

We extracted and reported the following study characteristics.

Tailoring process

We collated multiple reports related to the same study to gather information about the tailoring process. To characterise the tailoring process, if the included studies provided sufficient information, we classified determinants of professional practice into the seven domains of the TICD checklist: guideline factors, individual healthcare professional factors, patient factors, professional interactions, incentives and resources, capacity for organisational change, and social, political, and legal factors [18]. We used this determinant framework to enable comparison with the previous review update. Two review authors (MW, SF) independently classified determinants, and two additional authors (SMH, SOC) carried out a second review, with any discrepancies resolved through consultation with a third review author (FR, AOM).

Tailored implementation strategy

To characterise the tailored implementation strategy, one review author (MW) independently classified strategies based on the ERIC compilation of 73 implementation strategies [17], with a second review by another author (FR, SMH). We resolved any discrepancies through consultation with a third review author (SMH, AOM). We also explored whether studies set out the mechanisms of action underpinning the tailored implementation strategy. We coded ‘yes’ where studies included an explicit reference to mechanisms, included a logic model suggesting mechanisms of action or included a description of theoretical constructs targeted by the constituent strategies. We recorded as 'unclear' descriptions that were lacking detail or explicit references.

As part of the data extraction process, at least two authors (FR, JM, EM, DRA, LJM, AOM) independently extracted statistical data for healthcare professional practice outcomes. One author (FR or AOM) checked and entered data into a spreadsheet for analysis in consultation with EOR.

Risk of bias assessment in included studies

We assessed risk of bias for the outcomes of interest. Pairs of review authors (FR, JM, EM, RM, AOM) independently assessed risk of bias for each study using the Risk of bias version 2 (RoB 2) criteria outlined in the Cochrane Handbook for Systematic Reviews of Interventions [44]. Most included studies, and all of those included in the meta‐analysis, were cluster‐RCTs (including stepped‐wedge designs), so we applied the RoB 2 variant for cluster‐RCTs for these studies. We therefore assessed the additional domain (1b) relating to bias from timing of identification and recruitment of participants in relation to cluster‐randomisation, as recommended in the Cochrane Handbook (Section 23.1.2) [45]. For stepped‐wedge designs, we also considered potential confounding by time period and incomplete rollout of the intervention. Overall, we were interested in quantifying the effect of assignment to the tailored implementation strategy at baseline, regardless of whether the strategies were received as intended (the ‘intention‐to‐treat effect’). We resolved any disagreements by discussion or by involving a third review author (EM). We assessed the risk of bias according to the following domains:

bias arising from the randomisation process;bias due to deviations from intended interventions;bias due to missing outcome data;bias in measurement of the outcome;bias in selection of the reported result.

bias arising from the randomisation process;

bias arising from the randomisation process;

bias due to deviations from intended interventions;

bias due to deviations from intended interventions;

bias due to missing outcome data;

bias due to missing outcome data;

bias in measurement of the outcome;

bias in measurement of the outcome;

bias in selection of the reported result.

bias in selection of the reported result.

We summarised the risk of bias judgements across the studies included in the meta‐analysis for each of the domains listed. We assigned an overall risk of bias assessment to each of the studies in the meta‐analysis using the approach suggested in Chapter 8 of the Cochrane Handbook for Systematic Reviews of Interventions (Table 8.2.b) [44]. Specifically, we assigned a ‘low risk of bias’ if we judged the study to be at low risk of bias for all domains for the outcome in question. We assigned ‘some concerns’ if we judged the study to raise some concerns in at least one domain for this result, but not to be at high risk of bias for any domain. We assigned a ‘high risk of bias’ if we judged the study to be at high risk of bias in at least one domain for this outcome or to have some concerns for multiple domains in a way that substantially lowered confidence in the result. We did not exclude studies on the grounds of their risk of bias. The risk of bias in each of the included studies is presented in the Results section. Detailed responses to the RoB 2 signalling questions, and consensus domain‐level judgements for each included study, are available in the risk of bias tables in Supplementary material 6.

Measures of treatment effect

Measures of the effect of implementation strategies on healthcare professional practice outcomes varied across strategies and settings. To enable pooled analyses, we used dichotomous (or categorised as binary) odds, risk and rate ratios (and confidence intervals), where the included studies reported such outcomes. Some studies reported continuous or count measures without reporting adjusted relative measures of effect. We considered it less appropriate and less feasible to standardise and pool these measures because there is no agreed core outcome measure of professional practice. Also, due to the extensive heterogeneity in outcomes and scales used, continuous outcome measures were less amenable to standardisation. For example, studies reported mean differences in a composite guideline adherence score with different underlying components contributing to the score (using different numerical scales and ranges of possible scores). We extracted and reported continuous or count measures according to the Synthesis Without Meta‐analysis (SWiM) guidance [46].

Review author, EOR, performed meta‐analyses RevMan to evaluate the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies compared to non‐tailored strategies, and compared to no strategy [40].

We developed and applied decision rules to select measures of effect. If a study reported multiple outcome measures, we prioritised selecting cluster‐adjusted ratios of the intervention effect. First, the primary outcome if available; if not, a secondary outcome measure of healthcare professional practice. If the study reported results for multiple items pertaining to healthcare professionals' practice, then we selected measures based on the data collection method or source of data considered most valid by the author team, based on discussion between two or more review authors. If multiple valid measures were available, we selected the outcomes where the effect of the implementation strategy was closest and furthest from the null. This somewhat arbitrary approach circumvents the need for multiple sensitivity analyses that replace each outcome simultaneously across several studies. In addition, it protects against favourable selection of results and suspicion of bias. We used the most conservative effect measures (closest to the null) in the main meta‐analysis, and we present a separate meta‐analysis using the least conservative effect measure (furthest from the null) as part of the sensitivity analysis.

Only results at follow‐up were extracted and included in the meta‐analysis. In addition to adjustment for cluster design, some studies adjusted the effect measures reported for baseline variables when randomisation may not have achieved optimal balance. For studies with multiple eligible intervention arms compared to a single control arm, our protocol specified that we would include each arm and divide the control group. In practice, however, we did not have the data to apply this rule to the only study that had more than one intervention (Simon 2005 [47]). In the main analysis, we chose the intervention that was consistent with Baker and colleagues (group academic detailing versus control comparison) [12]. We also examined the individual academic detailing versus control comparison, which did not materially change the pooled estimate. Academic detailing is a form of continuing medical education that involves educational outreach by trained health professionals to support clinicians to incorporate evidence into their practice.

Unit of analysis issues

Cluster‐RCTs that are analysed in a manner that does not account for lack of independence across members of a cluster will have confidence intervals that are too narrow and are unsuitable for meta‐analysis [48]. In this review, an inclusion criterion for meta‐analysis was an effect measure (ratio) from analyses that adjusted for the cluster design, thereby minimising unit‐of‐analysis error.

Dealing with missing data

We documented any evidence of potential selective reporting or incomplete reporting of study data in the risk of bias tables. Overall, we randomised 22 of the 23 studies included in the meta‐analysis for the main comparison (tailored versus non‐tailored strategies) at the cluster level, for example, at the level of the clinic or general practice. In addition, we randomised 23 of the 24 studies in the meta‐analysis that compared tailored strategies against no strategy at the cluster level. Five of the studies described dropouts at this level during the study period (Looijmans 2010 [49, 50], Goodfellow 2016 [51, 52], Engers 2005 [53, 54, 55], Coenen 2004 [56], Lakshminarayan 2010 [57]), or failure of some clusters to collect outcome data (Fretheim 2006 [58, 59, 60]), of which one conducted a sensitivity analysis to check the impact of dropouts (Goodfellow 2016), and two confirmed that characteristics of dropouts and non‐dropouts were similar (Coenen 2004; Engers 2005).

We used effect measures where available and contacted study authors if we required information for meta‐analyses (e.g. denominator for groups). Given the number of studies involved, we did not contact study authors to obtain unreported outcome data. Issues with missing data were considered as part of the risk of bias assessment and sensitivity analyses detailed below. We reported the level of missingness and considered how that might impact the certainty of the evidence.

Reporting bias assessment

We conducted a sensitive search of major biomedical databases and trials registries (see Search methods for identification of studies). As the studies included in the review spanned approximately 10 years since the previous review and were not all recent publications, time‐lag bias is unlikely to be a major problem. We created and examined a funnel plot to explore possible publication biases, interpreting the results with caution [61].

Synthesis methods

Where possible, we included extracted relative results (odds ratios (ORs), risk ratios (RRs), rate ratios) in a random‐effects meta‐analysis using RevMan [40], with the aim of providing an overall assessment of the effectiveness of tailored strategies in comparison to either non‐tailored strategies or no strategy. We present the effects of strategies grouped according to comparison:

tailored implementation strategy versus non‐tailored implementation strategy;tailored implementation strategy versus no implementation strategy.

tailored implementation strategy versus non‐tailored implementation strategy;

tailored implementation strategy versus non‐tailored implementation strategy;

tailored implementation strategy versus no implementation strategy.

tailored implementation strategy versus no implementation strategy.

Although relative measures of association such as ORs and RRs only converge when an outcome is rare or when there is no association, we did not stratify on type of measurement because the majority of studies reported ORs and because the variation in pooled results is less likely attributable to the differences in effect measures, and more likely arises from differences in the interventions. We prioritised the results from the meta‐analysis as the most preferable method of synthesis as it provides both the direction of effect and the average effect of strategies across trials.

Where the data were unsuitable for pooling in meta‐analyses due to missing or incomplete data, we conducted the synthesis using vote‐counting approaches based on direction of effect following the procedures outlined in the Cochrane Handbook for Systematic Reviews of Interventions [62], and the SWiM guidelines [46]. We used a vote‐counting approach based on the direction of effect, rather than statistical significance, to summarise the findings across studies. This approach aligns with Cochrane best practice guidance, as focusing on statistical significance alone can be misleading due to issues such as low statistical power or arbitrary P value thresholds. By emphasising the direction of effects, we provide a more robust synthesis of the evidence when meta‐analysis is not possible. We presented an overview of these findings and a summary of intervention effects in a table.

Investigation of heterogeneity and subgroup analysis

We used the I² statistic to measure heterogeneity between the studies included in each analysis [63, 64]. In addition, we included prespecified subgroup analysis to assess whether the effect of the interventions was more homogeneous within subgroups. To check for differences across subgroups, we used the Q‐statistic, a weighted sum of the squared difference between the overall effect and each of the subgroup pooled effects.

We explored subgroup differences through prespecified analysis.

Equity‐related assessment

As part of the planned subgroup analysis outlined above, we examined whether the effect of tailored implementation strategies was moderated by study setting. We used the World Bank Classification of high‐, middle‐ or low‐income countries to classify the study setting [41].

Sensitivity analysis

We carried out sensitivity analyses, defined in the protocol, to assess the robustness of our conclusions and explore the impact on effect sizes, which involved restricting the analysis to studies with a low risk of bias [65]. Given the range of outcomes reported across studies to represent professional practice, we also performed a sensitivity analysis using the least conservative effect measures. We had originally planned to restrict the analysis to published studies, however in practice, it was not possible to include conference abstracts in the review because they contained insufficient information to judge whether tailoring had occurred or whether effect measures were adjusted for clustering.

Certainty of the evidence assessment

We summarised the findings in a summary of findings table for the main comparison only (tailored versus non‐tailored implementation strategies, minimum effect size) because this provided the most informative and conservative estimate of effect. Two review authors (FR, AOM) independently assessed the certainty of the evidence for the primary outcome (high, moderate, low, and very low) using the five GRADE considerations (risk of bias, consistency of effect, imprecision, indirectness, and publication bias). We used methods and recommendations described in Chapter 14 of the Cochrane Handbook forSystematic Reviews of interventions [66], and GRADEpro GDT software [67]. We resolved disagreements on certainty ratings by discussion and provided justification for decisions using footnotes in the table to aid readers' understanding of the review. Where necessary, we consulted a third review author (EM). We used plain language statements to report these findings in the review. We did not conduct GRADE assessments for the comparison with no strategy or the sensitivity analysis using maximum effect size, as these were additional analyses conducted to update and explore the review findings, but were not considered the primary focus of our review.

Consumer involvement

We sought feedback on the plain language summary from a panel of six members of the public, who had previously provided feedback on other research conducted at the university of the lead authors. The panel involved members of the public who are regular users of health services, and all had at least one doctor‐diagnosed chronic disease. The panel members were all female and white Irish. Panel members were reimbursed for the time spent reviewing the document in line with published payment guidance [68]. Suggestions for enhancing the clarity and accessibility of the language were incorporated into the draft version of the summary. The revised version was then reviewed one final time by a subset of three of the panel members. Participation in the final review of this summary was determined by the panel members’ availability, interest, and ability to return feedback within the required time frame.

Results of the search

For this review update, following the de‐duplication of search results, we assessed 8198 unique citations identified through databases (as shown in the PRISMA 2020 flow diagram, Figure) and reviewed the full text of 1022 articles. We also screened 3106 trials registers. Lastly, we reviewed the 32 studies that had been included in the previous version of the review and four studies that were awaiting classification at that time.

Figure 1: PRISMA diagram

On assessment, we found the following:

50 new studies from database searches that met the inclusion criteria2 studies from trials registers27 studies from the previous review that met the inclusion criteria. We excluded five studies from the previous review as they did not meet the definition for tailoring (Karuza 1995 [69]; Matchar 2002 [70, 71, 72]), or they reported patient outcomes only (Murphy 2009 [73, 74]; Van Bruggen 2008 [75]; Zwarenstein 2007 [76]), see Supplementary material 3.29 ongoing studies, that is, studies that meet the eligibility criteria, but which are still in progress and have no results available (see Supplementary material 5 for a list of ongoing studies). Studies with an unknown status in trials registers (e.g. Maaloe 2022 [77]; Yaya 2018 [78, 79]), where it was not possible to determine whether the study had been completed, were also assigned to this list.933 articles that did not meet the inclusion criteria and were therefore excluded (480 full texts with reasons provided in Supplementary material 3, with the remaining articles excluded with quick reference to the full text).4 studies awaiting classification as part of the previous review were excluded: Dixon 2013 [80]; Dizon 2014 [81, 82]; He 2014 [83]; McNulty 2014 [84].

Clinician ordering of out‐of‐office BP monitoringRR 2.22 (95% CI 0.78 to 6.28; P = 0.12)YYYIn this cluster randomized trial, a theory‐informed multifaceted implementation strategy targeting patient‐, clinician‐, and systems‐level barriers increased out‐of‐office BP monitoring in patients with elevated office BP without a prior diagnosis of hypertension. These improvements were driven by increases in ABPM [ambulatory blood pressure monitoring] ordering and completion as well as increases in HBPM [home blood pressure monitoring] completion among those with HBPM ordered. In contrast, no patients had ABPM ordered or completed in usual care practices. These findings are consistent with the low use of ABPM in US‐based primary care settings and support that increasing the accessibility of ABPM, paired with education and reminders, can increase uptake of ABPM in primary care, at least marginally. The value of out‐of‐office BP monitoring was confirmed, as approximately half of patients who completed out‐of‐office BP monitoring were diagnosed with white‐coat hypertension and potentially avoided inappropriate treatment and chronic disease labelling.Llewelyn 20221. Provision of pre‐conception care (PCC)The number of provided PCC‐consultations was comparable before and after the intervention (P = 0.760).NNYThis study shows that a locally tailored PCC‐intervention containing a social marketing strategy to encourage prospective parents to actively prepare for pregnancy has the potential to improve preconceptional lifestyle behaviours. The intervention showed some tentative positive effects on lifestyle behaviours among prospective parents, knowledge and competence among healthcare providers and reaching the target population online. However, the sample size in this study was small and health behaviour changes were marginal. Nevertheless, the results of this study contribute to the evidence regarding the barriers and facilitators for implementing PCC‐interventions to optimize the health of prospective parents and future generations.Llewelyn 20231. DDDs per admission for specific antibiotic groups"Adjusting for the effects of COVID‐19 and interrupted time series trends (shown by site in appendix pp 51–127), the intervention was associated with a –1.0% (95% CI –4.0 to 2.1) immediate change in total antibiotic DDDs per admission and a sustained –4.8% (95% CI –9.1 to –0.2) change per year subsequently (figure 3, 4), with little association between the immediate and longer‐term intervention effects across sites (r = –0.088, P = 0.60; appendix p 128).""Adjusted models showed no evidence of an immediate effect on total antibiotic DDDs per bed‐day (–0.4%, 95% CI –3.2 to 2.5), with weak evidence of subsequent reductions (by –4.2% per year (95% CI –8.3 to 0.1); figure 3; appendix pp 13, 130).""At implementation, rates of broadspectrum and Watch DDDs per admission decreased significantly (–6.3%, 95% CI –9.5 to –3.0 for broadspectrum DDDs and –9.5% (95% CI –13.2 to –5.6 for Watch DDDs), as did quinolones (–15.5%, 95% CI –21.9 to –8.5), whereas Access DDDs per admission increased slightly (4.4%, 95% CI 0.3 to 8.8; figure 3; appendix pp 131–137, 140–144). We did not identify evidence of immediate effects on other secondary antibiotic outcomes at implementation, nor on piperacillin–tazobactam DDDs per admission (figure 3). There were sustained reductions in year‐on‐year trends for most antibiotic outcomes, including narrow‐spectrum (–5.2%, 95% CI –9.4 to –0.9; figure 3; appendix pp 131–132), Watch (–11.0%, 95% CI –17.1 to –4.5), Access (–5.3%, 95% CI –10.0 to –0.4; appendix pp 133–137), and oral antibiotics (–6.4%, 95% CI –11.2 to –1.4; appendix pp 138–139). We did not identify evidence of long‐term effects on broad‐spectrum antibiotics (–2.6%, 95% CI –8.5 to 3.6; appendix p 131), parenteral antibiotics (–0.9%, 95% CI –5.2 to 3.6; appendix p 138), and antibiotics considered Access or Watch depending on indication (1.0, 95% CI –5.2 to 7.6; appendix p 133). "Adjusting for the effects of COVID‐19 and interrupted time series trends (shown by site in appendix pp 51–127), the intervention was associated with a –1.0% (95% CI –4.0 to 2.1) immediate change in total antibiotic DDDs per admission and a sustained –4.8% (95% CI –9.1 to –0.2) change per year subsequently (figure 3, 4), with little association between the immediate and longer‐term intervention effects across sites (r = –0.088, P = 0.60; appendix p 128)."

"Adjusting for the effects of COVID‐19 and interrupted time series trends (shown by site in appendix pp 51–127), the intervention was associated with a –1.0% (95% CI –4.0 to 2.1) immediate change in total antibiotic DDDs per admission and a sustained –4.8% (95% CI –9.1 to –0.2) change per year subsequently (figure 3, 4), with little association between the immediate and longer‐term intervention effects across sites (r = –0.088, P = 0.60; appendix p 128)."

"Adjusted models showed no evidence of an immediate effect on total antibiotic DDDs per bed‐day (–0.4%, 95% CI –3.2 to 2.5), with weak evidence of subsequent reductions (by –4.2% per year (95% CI –8.3 to 0.1); figure 3; appendix pp 13, 130)."

"Adjusted models showed no evidence of an immediate effect on total antibiotic DDDs per bed‐day (–0.4%, 95% CI –3.2 to 2.5), with weak evidence of subsequent reductions (by –4.2% per year (95% CI –8.3 to 0.1); figure 3; appendix pp 13, 130)."

"At implementation, rates of broadspectrum and Watch DDDs per admission decreased significantly (–6.3%, 95% CI –9.5 to –3.0 for broadspectrum DDDs and –9.5% (95% CI –13.2 to –5.6 for Watch DDDs), as did quinolones (–15.5%, 95% CI –21.9 to –8.5), whereas Access DDDs per admission increased slightly (4.4%, 95% CI 0.3 to 8.8; figure 3; appendix pp 131–137, 140–144). We did not identify evidence of immediate effects on other secondary antibiotic outcomes at implementation, nor on piperacillin–tazobactam DDDs per admission (figure 3). There were sustained reductions in year‐on‐year trends for most antibiotic outcomes, including narrow‐spectrum (–5.2%, 95% CI –9.4 to –0.9; figure 3; appendix pp 131–132), Watch (–11.0%, 95% CI –17.1 to –4.5), Access (–5.3%, 95% CI –10.0 to –0.4; appendix pp 133–137), and oral antibiotics (–6.4%, 95% CI –11.2 to –1.4; appendix pp 138–139). We did not identify evidence of long‐term effects on broad‐spectrum antibiotics (–2.6%, 95% CI –8.5 to 3.6; appendix p 131), parenteral antibiotics (–0.9%, 95% CI –5.2 to 3.6; appendix p 138), and antibiotics considered Access or Watch depending on indication (1.0, 95% CI –5.2 to 7.6; appendix p 133). By contrast, year‐on‐year trends in DDDs per admissions increased faster after versus before implementation for carbapenems (12.3%, 95% CI 2.3 to 23.2; appendix pp 140–144) with a similar trend for Reserve antibiotics (7.3%, 95% CI –1.5 to 17.0; appendix pp 133–137), but from low levels and with wide CIs (table; appendix pp 48–50)."

aBold text indicates outcomes that were designated as primary outcomes in the original studies. bUse of theory or theoretical framework was coded as “Yes” here if a study explicitly reported use of either a formal theory or a framework, whereas in the Characteristics of included studies (Supplementary material 2), theory and framework are reported separately.

A total of 34 potentially eligible studies were completed on a trials register since this review was finalised. Seven of these studies had peer‐reviewed results papers since the completion of the updated search and were included in the list of studies awaiting classification (Bartels 2024 [85, 86]; Blecker 2025 [87, 88, 89]; Coronado 2025 [90, 91, 92, 93, 94, 95, 96]; Mello 2018 [97, 98, 99]; Naef 2025 [100, 101]; Polk 2021 [102]; Wright 2025 [103, 104, 105]). Because these results became available after data extraction and synthesis were completed, it was not feasible to incorporate them into the current version of the review. These studies are not considered likely to substantially alter the overall conclusions but should be prioritised for consideration and inclusion in a future review update. In some cases, completed studies had reported results in other formats (e.g. preprints, PhD dissertations or grey literature) rather than in peer‐reviewed publications and were therefore also retained in the awaiting classification list (see Supplementary material 4 for the characteristics of studies awaiting classification).

Included studies

The total number of included studies was 79 (Table; Table; Figure), of which 47 were included in the meta‐analysis. Overall, there were 60 cluster‐RCTs, 15 stepped‐wedge cluster‐RCTs, and four RCTs (Avorn 1983 [106]; De Ruijter 2018 [107, 108, 109, 110, 111]; Hux 1999 [112]; Yellappa 2018 [113, 114, 115]), see Supplementary material 2 for Characteristics of included studies. Note, we re‐classified Hux 1999 as a parallel‐group RCT rather than a cluster‐RCT.

Healthcare setting and characteristics of healthcare professionals

Most studies were undertaken in the USA (n = 19) or the Netherlands (n = 13). Most were conducted in high‐income (n = 69) or upper middle‐income countries (n = 2), with only eight conducted in lower‐middle or low‐income countries (Democratic Republic of the Congo, Zambia, Mozambique, Uganda, Tanzania, Indonesia, Kenya and India).

Thirty‐one studies were based in primary care settings, 18 in inpatient hospital settings, six in outpatient settings, six in mixed (primary and secondary care) settings, and 16 in other settings (including nursing homes, physiotherapy, and chiropractic practices, a mix of general practices and older adult care organisations, private pharmacies in the community).

The healthcare professionals included in the studies were mixed professional groups in 47 studies, physicians in 22 studies, nurses in five, pharmacists in one, and ‘other’ in four studies. Of the 79 included studies, only 41 reported participant numbers. Participant numbers were not reported or were unclear in 38 studies, most often because they provided only cluster counts, or reporting was unclear or incomplete. Based on the studies that clearly reported participant numbers, 25,630 healthcare professional participants were included, though the total number of participants is expected to be higher because the exact number of healthcare professionals involved was unclear in the remaining 38 of the 79 studies.

Disaggregated by comparison:

35 studies involving a total of 5015 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored implementation intervention on professional practice compared to a non‐tailored intervention;44 studies with a total of 20,615 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored intervention on professional practice compared to no intervention.

35 studies involving a total of 5015 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored implementation intervention on professional practice compared to a non‐tailored intervention;

35 studies involving a total of 5015 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored implementation intervention on professional practice compared to a non‐tailored intervention;

44 studies with a total of 20,615 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored intervention on professional practice compared to no intervention.

44 studies with a total of 20,615 healthcare professionals examined the effect of a tailored intervention on professional practice compared to no intervention.

Targeted behaviours

We noted substantial variation in the number and type of outcome measures included in each study. Overall, the 79 studies reported 378 outcome measures of professional practice (193 primary, 214 secondary), 298 (73%) of which were dichotomous outcome measures, 81 (20%) were continuous outcome measures, and 28 (7%) were count data. Measures varied with respect to the type of professional practice behaviour; most focused on prescribing (n = 146) (Figure).

Figure 2. Professional practice behaviours reported by included studies. The figure shows the distribution of different measures of professional practice behaviours across studies included in the review. ‘Other’ includes outcomes that could not be categorised into the main groups, for example, bed occupancy rate, time spent in a clinical department, discussion of patient cases within a multidisciplinary team, use of a new software system, and frequency of consultations. Figure created by review authors

Studies assessed outcomes in a variety of ways, including via abstraction from medical records, considered the most ‘valid’ approach (e.g. Lakshminarayan 2010), questionnaires, or checklists (e.g. Engers 2005; French 2022 [116, 117, 118]). Examples of how professional practice was measured include the following.

Determinants of practice

Most studies used interviews (n = 40), surveys (n = 32), focus groups (n = 38), or observations or ethnographic methods (n = 4), with professionals or patients to identify determinants or select strategies. Twenty‐six studies used other methods such as brainstorming and workshops.

Eleven studies did not provide sufficient detail on the determinants of practice to enable categorisation according to the TICD framework (Avorn 1983; Avorn 1992 [131, 132]; Emond 2022 [133, 134]; Gulliford 2019; Hux 1999; Kakande 2022 [135, 136]; King 2021 [137]; Langham 2002 [138]; Llewelyn 2022 [139, 140, 141]; Van Gaal 2010 [142, 143, 144, 145]; Van Uum 2020 [146, 147]). At the TICD domain level, individual healthcare professional factors were the more commonly reported determinants, noted in 64 studies. Incentives and resources were noted in 50 studies. Professional interactions were reported in 34 studies and patient factors in 33 studies. Guideline factors were noted in 26 studies; feasibility (10 studies), compatibility (7 studies) and accessibility of the recommendation (6 studies) were most coded as determinants in that domain. Organisational capacity was noted in 23 studies and social, political and legal factors in 12 studies.

Inputs used in the tailoring process

The use of theory, models or theoretical frameworks as part of the tailoring process was referenced in 42 studies, the use of evidence of the effectiveness of strategies was referenced in 35 studies and input from stakeholders was referenced in 33 studies. Sixteen studies drew on all three elements (Bosch 2019 [148, 149, 150, 151, 152]; Brown 2018; Cadilhac 2022 [153, 154]; Coombs 2021 [155, 156, 157]; Doherty 2022 [158, 159, 160]; Emond 2022; Etxeberria 2018 [161, 162]; Flottorp 2002 [163, 164, 165]; French 2022; Roos‐Blom 2019 [166, 167, 168]; Guthrie 2016 [169, 170]; Jäger 2017; Kronish 2023 [171, 172, 173, 174]; LaBresh 2014 [175, 176]; Looijmans 2010; Sussman 2018 [177]).

Characteristics of the intervention

The tailored strategies applied in the intervention arms were generally multifaceted (n = 76), that is, they involved more than one component. For example, Aakhus 2016 [178, 179, 180, 181] reported 52 individual activities. These activities could be categorised into four broad ERIC strategies: develop a formal implementation blueprint, develop educational materials, conduct educational outreach visits and distribute educational materials.

In terms of ERIC strategies, 'distribute educational materials' (in the form of guidelines, copies of articles, summary documents, e‐learning packages; 46 studies), 'audit and provide feedback' (42 studies), and 'conduct educational meetings', including online meetings (39 studies), were the most common implementation strategies. In 26 studies, educational outreach was used, either on a one‐to‐one basis or with groups. In 24 studies, reminders were used, either in consultations (frequently generated by computer systems) or in mailings or meetings. The average number of ERIC strategies used in a study was five (median = 4 strategies), with the number ranging from 1 to 10 strategies within a study. Three studies used 10 or more strategies (LaBresh 2014; Lewis 2022 [182, 183, 184, 185, 186]; Scholl 2021 [187, 188]).

Studies delineated four additional strategies, which we considered not to be adequately captured within the ERIC taxonomy.

'Provide patient educational materials' (n = 20; Bosch 2019; De Ruijter 2018; Engers 2005; Flottorp 2002; French 2022; Garg 2024 [189, 190, 191]; Hartman 2023 [192, 193, 194, 195]; Van Lieshout 2016 [196, 197, 198, 199, 200]; Jäger 2017; Kenyon 2017 [201]; Kronish 2023; LaBresh 2014; Llewelyn 2023 [202, 203]; Rosario 2022 [204, 205]; Scholl 2021; Huffman 2018 [206, 207, 208]; Van Gaal 2010; Van Uum 2020; Yellappa 2018; Zou 2016 [209, 210, 211, 212]). This included providing healthcare professionals with leaflets, decision aids, posters, and online information sources to distribute among patients or to be distributed directly to patients.'Provide tools and materials to support implementation' (n = 12; Arnold 2021 [213, 214]; Bosch 2019; Betrán 2018 [215, 216, 217, 218]; De Ruijter 2018; Engers 2005; Figueiras 2006 [219]; Foy 2004 [220]; Fretheim 2006; Goodwin 2001 [221, 222]; Huffman 2018; Soumerai 1998 [223, 224]; Yellappa 2018). This included activities such as providing standardised forms, decision trees and flowcharts.'Computerised decision support system', although proposed as a subcomponent of 'Remind clinicians' in the ERIC taxonomy, it was coded as a distinct category in six studies as the system went beyond a simple reminder (Flottorp 2002; LaBresh 2014; Llewelyn 2023; Luitjes 2018 [225, 226]; Sussman 2018; Vesco 2021 [227, 228]). For example, in the case of Sussman 2018, it concerned cardiovascular risk estimation based on various individual risk factors.'Design a referral pathway' (n = 1; Llewelyn 2022).

'Provide patient educational materials' (n = 20; Bosch 2019; De Ruijter 2018; Engers 2005; Flottorp 2002; French 2022; Garg 2024 [189, 190, 191]; Hartman 2023 [192, 193, 194, 195]; Van Lieshout 2016 [196, 197, 198, 199, 200]; Jäger 2017; Kenyon 2017 [201]; Kronish 2023; LaBresh 2014; Llewelyn 2023 [202, 203]; Rosario 2022 [204, 205]; Scholl 2021; Huffman 2018 [206, 207, 208]; Van Gaal 2010; Van Uum 2020; Yellappa 2018; Zou 2016 [209, 210, 211, 212]). This included providing healthcare professionals with leaflets, decision aids, posters, and online information sources to distribute among patients or to be distributed directly to patients.

Some descriptions of implementation strategies also included processes reflective of ongoing tailoring (that is, identification of emergent barriers and selection of solutions) as part of the intervention. Classified according to the ERIC compilation, these are:

'Assess for readiness and identify barriers and facilitators' (n = 7; Cadilhac 2022; Goodfellow 2016; Lakshminarayan 2010; Scott 2013 [229, 230]; Simon 2005; Sinnema 2015 [231, 232, 233]; Soumerai 1998);'Capture and share local knowledge' (n = 2; LaBresh 2014; Simon 2005);'Conduct local needs assessment' (n = 2; Emond 2022; Lewis 2022); andexplicit references to ‘Tailor strategies’ within broader, multifaceted interventions (n = 8; Cadilhac 2022; Cheater 2006 [234]; Lewis 2022; Llewelyn 2022; Morrison 2015 [235, 236, 237]; Perez 2015 [238, 239]; Rodrigues 2019 [240, 241]; Schouten 2007).
