explicit references to ‘Tailor strategies’ within broader, multifaceted interventions (n = 8; Cadilhac 2022; Cheater 2006 [234]; Lewis 2022; Llewelyn 2022; Morrison 2015 [235, 236, 237]; Perez 2015 [238, 239]; Rodrigues 2019 [240, 241]; Schouten 2007).

Although limited, five studies included implementation strategies that related more to preparation than to implementing the intervention and would be expected to enhance rather than drive implementation, for example, 'Build a coalition' (Bosch 2019; Llewelyn 2022), 'Create a learning collaborative' (De Ruijter 2018; Emond 2022; Garg 2024; Kakande 2022; Morrison 2015).

Twenty‐eight studies explicitly specified the mechanisms by which the implementation intervention was intended to work, for example, providing a logic model. Five studies had more than one tailored implementation intervention arm (Avorn 1983; Cheater 2006; Langham 2002; Santoso 1996 [242]; Simon 2005), two of which could be included in the meta‐analysis (Cheater 2006; Simon 2005).

Contact with study authors

We attempted to contact the authors of nine included studies to obtain additional information needed to calculate design effects (e.g. intra‐cluster correlation coefficients or mean cluster sizes) or effect measures adjusted for clustering. We received responses from three studies (Cheater 2006; Kapoor 2020 [243, 244]; Rosario 2022). In each case, study authors confirmed aspects of the cluster design or clarified the effect measures reported, or both. However, they did not provide any additional effect estimates suitable for meta‐analysis. We received no response from the remaining six studies, or we were unable to identify valid contact information (Althabe 2019 [245, 246, 247]; Beeckman 2013 [248]; Callahan 1994 [249, 250]; LaBresh 2014; Leviton 1999 [251, 252]; Yellappa 2018). We presented details of study author contact attempts in Supplementary material 2. None of the study authors provided any unpublished data, and we conducted all analyses using data available from published reports or trials registry records.

Excluded studies

We excluded 932 studies because they did not meet our eligibility criteria. Of these, 480 were excluded for specific reasons, while the remaining studies were excluded based on quick reference to the full text, which determined that the study was not related to tailoring. For details, see Supplementary material 3 for Characteristics of excluded studies.

Risk of bias in included studies

Results of the risk of bias assessment are summarised for the outcomes included in the meta‐analysis (see Supplementary material 6, Figure; Figure). The following summary includes the selected outcome from all studies contributing to either of the two quantitative comparisons evaluated in this review (tailored vs non‐tailored strategy, and tailored vs no strategy). There was no overlap in studies between these two comparisons, and no important differences in the distribution of risk of bias judgements were observed between them; they are therefore described together. Furthermore, as only one outcome (impact on professional practice) was included in our review, we did not analyse or present the results separately by the specific type of outcome measure that assessed professional practice. Thus, the judgements reported in this section include all dichotomous measures of this single outcome included in the review’s main comparisons.

Note: Results reported for Simon 2005 are group academic detailing vs control [47]. We included this group detailing comparison in the main analysis, consistent with Baker 2001 [1]. We also tested the effect of individual academic detailing vs control (OR 1.30, 95% CI 0.95 to 1.79), which did not change the pooled estimate (this study contributed 7% of the weight to the pooled OR).

Note: Effect estimates are labelled as odds ratios for consistency across the forest plot. However, some included studies originally reported other effect measures. In this analysis, French 2022, Frethaim 2006 and Zou 2016 reported a risk ratio [58, 116, 209], while Kakande 2022, Perez 2015 and van Uum 2020 reported a rate ratio [135, 146, 238]. All other studies in this plot reported odds ratios.

Note: Effect estimates are labelled as odds ratios for consistency across the forest plot. However, some included studies originally reported other effect measures. In this analysis, Brown 2018, Kenyon 2017, Kronish 2023, and Scott 2013 reported a risk ratio [119, 171, 201, 229], while Garg 2024, Gulliford 2019, and Hartman 2023 reported a rate ratio [128, 189, 192]. All other studies in this plot reported odds ratios.

All studies included in the meta‐analysis were cluster‐RCTs (including stepped‐wedge cluster‐RCTs), and we used the RoB 2 variant for cluster‐RCTs to assess their risk of bias.

Overall, we assessed the risk of bias as high in four studies (Callahan 1994; Engers 2005; Looijmans 2010; Sussman 2018), with ‘some concerns’ in 38 studies and as low in the remaining five studies (Brown 2018; Garg 2024; Guthrie 2016; Scott 2013; Zou 2016).

The overall ‘high’ judgement was determined by a high risk of bias reported in domain 1A (‘Bias arising from the randomisation process’) for one study (Sussman 2018), domain 3 (‘Bias due to missing outcome data’) for one study (Looijmans 2010), and domain 4 (‘Bias in measurement of the outcome’) for two studies (Callahan 1994; Engers 2005).

The biases that we identified the most often across studies were:

bias in the measurement of the outcome (2 outcomes rated high, 6 outcomes rated some concerns);bias arising from the randomisation process (1 outcome rated high, 16 outcomes rated some concerns);bias due to missing outcome data (1 outcome rated high, 6 outcomes rated some concerns);bias in the selection of the reported result (25 outcomes rated as some concerns); andbias due to deviations from intended interventions (31 outcomes rated as some concerns).

bias in the measurement of the outcome (2 outcomes rated high, 6 outcomes rated some concerns);

bias in the measurement of the outcome (2 outcomes rated high, 6 outcomes rated some concerns);

bias arising from the randomisation process (1 outcome rated high, 16 outcomes rated some concerns);

bias arising from the randomisation process (1 outcome rated high, 16 outcomes rated some concerns);

bias due to missing outcome data (1 outcome rated high, 6 outcomes rated some concerns);

bias due to missing outcome data (1 outcome rated high, 6 outcomes rated some concerns);

bias in the selection of the reported result (25 outcomes rated as some concerns); and

bias in the selection of the reported result (25 outcomes rated as some concerns); and

bias due to deviations from intended interventions (31 outcomes rated as some concerns).

bias due to deviations from intended interventions (31 outcomes rated as some concerns).

Where we chose separate outcomes for the most and least conservative effect sizes from any of the included studies, the risk of bias rating was the same for both outcomes within those studies.

Risk of bias arising from the randomisation process (domain 1A)

One of the included studies was at high risk of bias with regard to domain 1A (Sussman 2018). This was because they did not provide information on allocation concealment.

Overall, we classified 16 of the included studies as having 'some concerns' in domain 1A, mainly due to a lack of information on how randomisation was performed or because there were some baseline differences between groups (Avorn 1992; Baker 2001 [1]; Beeckman 2013; Betrán 2018; Callahan 1994; Flottorp 2002; Kronish 2023; Lakshminarayan 2010; Leviton 1999; Lewis 2022; Looijmans 2010; Schouten 2007; Sharp 2017 [253, 254]; Simon 2005; Vesco 2021; Voorn 2017 [255, 256, 257]). The remaining 30 included studies were at low risk of bias for domain 1A.

Risk of bias arising from the timing of identification or recruitment of participants in a cluster‐randomised trial (domain 1B)

No outcomes included in the meta‐analysis were at high risk of bias in domain 1B.

Six outcomes had a rating of some concerns, mainly due to a lack of information, either to judge whether all participants had been identified and recruited before the clusters were randomised or if selection of individual participants was affected by knowledge of the intervention (Avorn 1992, Hartman 2023; Huffman 2018; Lewis 2022; Simon 2005; Van Uum 2020). In some studies, recruitment appeared to happen after allocation (Hartman 2023; Lewis 2022; Van Uum 2020), or there was some evidence of imbalances in baseline characteristics (Lewis 2022).

The remaining 41 outcomes were at low risk of bias for domain 1B.

Deviations from the intended interventions (effect of assignment to intervention and effect of adhering to intervention) (domain 2)

We did not rate any outcomes at high risk for this domain.

Overall, 31 outcomes had a rating of some concerns, mainly due to lack of information to judge whether deviations arose because of the study context. In some cases, because some deviation was reported (e.g. due to lack of kits as part of the intervention (Betrán 2018), or change to intervals of intervention delivery (Doherty 2022)), or because some potential indication of contamination or change in behaviour in the control group as a result of being in the study was reported (Kenyon 2017, King 2021).

A total of 16 outcomes were at low risk of bias related to deviations from intended interventions (Brown 2018; Cadilhac 2022; Flottorp 2002; French 2022; Garg 2024; Goodfellow 2016; Gulliford 2019; Guthrie 2016; Van Lieshout 2016; Kronish 2023; Lakshminarayan 2010; Luitjes 2018; Scott 2013; Sussman 2018; Van Uum 2020; Zou 2016).

Risk of bias due to missing outcome data (domain 3)

Overall, one outcome was at high risk of bias for domain 3 (Looijmans 2010). This study reported dropouts of clusters from the study.

Overall, we rated six outcomes as having some concerns (Coenen 2004; Engers 2005; French 2022; Fretheim 2006; Goodfellow 2016; Voorn 2017), for reporting some incomplete outcome data, for example, or because missingness was unclear (Voorn 2017). In one case, the study authors reported an even distribution of missing data between groups (Fretheim 2006). In the case of French 2022, for these outcomes, data could only be collected in certain (urban) clusters as opposed to the full cohort. Some studies with outcomes rated as some concerns reported dropouts of clusters but performed sensitivity analysis to explore the effect of missing clusters (Goodfellow 2016), or confirmed that rates of dropouts and characteristics of those who dropped out and did not drop out were similar (Coenen 2004; Engers 2005).

A total of 40 outcomes were at low risk of bias related to missing outcome data.

Risk of bias in measurement of the outcome (domain 4)

Overall, two outcomes were at high risk of bias because of a lack of information on how the outcome was measured (Callahan 1994), or use of self‐report checklists or questionnaires completed by unblinded physicians (Engers 2005).

Overall, six outcomes had a rating of some concerns (Avorn 1992; Beeckman 2013; Doherty 2022; Lewis 2022; Luitjes 2018; Van Lieshout 2016). This was mainly due to lack of detail about who collected the data (Avorn 1992), collection by self‐report (Doherty 2022; Lewis 2022), or unblinded observation (Beeckman 2013).

A total of 39 outcomes were at low risk of bias related to outcome measurement.

Risk of bias in selection of the reported result (domain 5)

We did not rate any outcomes at high risk of bias related to the selection of the reported result.

Overall, 25 outcomes had a rating of some concerns, mainly due to the lack of a protocol or analysis plan against which to check the current analysis. For some studies, there were documented changes to the plan (Goodfellow 2016; Gulliford 2019; Van Lieshout 2016).

A total of 22 outcomes were at low risk of bias regarding selection of reported results.

Overall, we judged the majority of studies to be at low risk of bias or to have some concerns across most domains. This reflects both the generally robust design of the included studies (many of which were recent cluster‐RCTs conducted in pragmatic health system settings) and the use of RoB 2, which provides structured domain‐level judgements and reduces the frequency of 'unclear' ratings that were common in earlier reviews using the original RoB tool. Nonetheless, there were recurrent concerns across all domains, which may limit confidence in some findings.

For the continuous outcome measures synthesised using SWiM, we presented study‐level risk of bias judgements in Table, alongside the vote‐counting results. Although risk of bias was originally assessed at the outcome level for these studies, in line with Cochrane guidance, for the SWiM analysis, we used these outcome‐level assessments to derive an overall study‐level judgement. This study‐level judgement was then used to accompany the vote counting across outcomes, since the SWiM synthesis is conducted across all relevant outcomes reported in each study.

We did not identify evidence of immediate effects on other secondary antibiotic outcomes at implementation, nor on piperacillin–tazobactam DDDs per admission (figure 3). There were sustained reductions in year‐on‐year trends for most antibiotic outcomes, including narrow‐spectrum (–5·2%, 95% CI –9·4 to –0·9; figure 3; appendix pp 131–132), Watch (–11·0%, –17·1 to –4·5), Access (–5·3%, –10·0 to –0·4; appendix pp 133–137), and oral antibiotics (–6·4%, –11·2 to –1·4; appendix pp 138–139). We did not identify evidence of long‐term effects on broad‐spectrum antibiotics (–2·6%, 95% CI –8·5 to 3·6; appendix p 131), parenteral antibiotics (–0·9%, –5·2 to 3·6; appendix p 138), and antibiotics considered Access or Watch depending on indication (1·0, –5·2 to 7·6; appendix p 133). By contrast, year‐on‐year trends in DDDs per admissions increased faster after versus before implementation for carbapenems (12·3%, 2·3 to 23·2; appendix pp 140–144) with a similar trend for Reserve antibiotics (7·3%, –1·5 to 17·0; appendix pp 133–137), but from low levels and with wide CIs (table; appendix pp 48–50)."YbHighLlewelyn 20221.Provision of pre‐conception care (PCC)The number of provided pre‐conception care consultations was comparable before and after the intervention (P = 0.760)NSome concernsReynolds 20211. "Adjusting for the effects of COVID‐19 and interrupted time series trends (shown by site in appendix pp 51–127), the intervention was associated with a –1.0% (95% CI –4.0 to 2.1) immediate change in total antibiotic DDDs per admission and a sustained –4·8% (–9·1 to –0·2) change per year subsequently (figure 3, 4), with little association between the immediate and longer‐term intervention effects across sites (r=–0·088, p=0·60; appendix p 128)."

"Adjusting for the effects of COVID‐19 and interrupted time series trends (shown by site in appendix pp 51–127), the intervention was associated with a –1.0% (95% CI –4.0 to 2.1) immediate change in total antibiotic DDDs per admission and a sustained –4·8% (–9·1 to –0·2) change per year subsequently (figure 3, 4), with little association between the immediate and longer‐term intervention effects across sites (r=–0·088, p=0·60; appendix p 128)."

"Adjusted models showed no evidence of an immediate effect on total antibiotic DDDs per bed‐day (–0·4%, 95% CI –3·2 to 2·5), with weak evidence of subsequent reductions (by –4·2% per year, –8·3 to 0·1; figure 3; appendix pp 13, 130)."

"Adjusted models showed no evidence of an immediate effect on total antibiotic DDDs per bed‐day (–0·4%, 95% CI –3·2 to 2·5), with weak evidence of subsequent reductions (by –4·2% per year, –8·3 to 0·1; figure 3; appendix pp 13, 130)."

"At implementation, rates of broadspectrum and Watch DDDs per admission decreased significantly (–6·3%, 95% CI –9·5 to –3·0 for broadspectrum DDDs and –9·5%, –13·2 to –5·6 for Watch DDDs), as did quinolones (–15·5%, –21·9 to –8·5), whereas Access DDDs per admission increased slightly (4·4%, 0·3–8·8; figure 3; appendix pp 131–137, 140–144). We did not identify evidence of immediate effects on other secondary antibiotic outcomes at implementation, nor on piperacillin–tazobactam DDDs per admission (figure 3). There were sustained reductions in year‐on‐year trends for most antibiotic outcomes, including narrow‐spectrum (–5·2%, 95% CI –9·4 to –0·9; figure 3; appendix pp 131–132), Watch (–11·0%, –17·1 to –4·5), Access (–5·3%, –10·0 to –0·4; appendix pp 133–137), and oral antibiotics (–6·4%, –11·2 to –1·4; appendix pp 138–139). We did not identify evidence of long‐term effects on broad‐spectrum antibiotics (–2·6%, 95% CI –8·5 to 3·6; appendix p 131), parenteral antibiotics (–0·9%, –5·2 to 3·6; appendix p 138), and antibiotics considered Access or Watch depending on indication (1·0, –5·2 to 7·6; appendix p 133). By contrast, year‐on‐year trends in DDDs per admissions increased faster after versus before implementation for carbapenems (12·3%, 2·3 to 23·2; appendix pp 140–144) with a similar trend for Reserve antibiotics (7·3%, –1·5 to 17·0; appendix pp 133–137), but from low levels and with wide CIs (table; appendix pp 48–50)."

"At implementation, rates of broadspectrum and Watch DDDs per admission decreased significantly (–6·3%, 95% CI –9·5 to –3·0 for broadspectrum DDDs and –9·5%, –13·2 to –5·6 for Watch DDDs), as did quinolones (–15·5%, –21·9 to –8·5), whereas Access DDDs per admission increased slightly (4·4%, 0·3–8·8; figure 3; appendix pp 131–137, 140–144). We did not identify evidence of immediate effects on other secondary antibiotic outcomes at implementation, nor on piperacillin–tazobactam DDDs per admission (figure 3). We did not identify evidence of long‐term effects on broad‐spectrum antibiotics (–2·6%, 95% CI –8·5 to 3·6; appendix p 131), parenteral antibiotics (–0·9%, –5·2 to 3·6; appendix p 138), and antibiotics considered Access or Watch depending on indication (1·0, –5·2 to 7·6; appendix p 133). The remaining studies were not eligible for meta‐analysis because the data were not available in a suitable format. We used vote‐counting based on the direction of effect to synthesise the results of these studies (Table). See Supplementary material 9 for a list of studies not included in the meta‐analysis and the reasons for it.

Dichotomous outcomes

Tailored strategies versus non‐tailored strategies

In total, 35 studies compared tailored strategies to non‐tailored strategies. Of those studies, 23 were suitable for meta‐analysis; 20 had a primary outcome that was used in the meta‐analysis, while the remaining three had an outcome selected following the decision rules. The pooled odds ratio using the most conservative reported result suggested that tailored implementation strategies probably lead to a slight improvement in professional practice compared with non‐tailored strategies (OR 1.49, 95% CI 1.26 to 1.75; P < 0.00001; 23 studies, 2600 participants, moderate‐certainty evidence; Analysis 1.1; Figure). Although only four of the studies had an OR greater than 1, there was significant heterogeneity (I2 = 82%; P < 0.0001), as expected, due to the large OR found in some studies and the tight confidence intervals in others. The sensitivity analysis using the least conservative estimate of strategy effect did not materially change the results; the OR may be as high as 1.74, (95% CI 1.35 to 2.24; P < 0.0001; Analysis 1.2; Supplementary material 7).

Tailored strategies versus no strategy

Overall, 44 studies compared tailored strategies to no strategy. Of those studies, 24 were suitable for inclusion in the meta‐analysis. The pooled OR was 1.34 (95% CI 1.17 to 1.53; P < 0.0001; 24 studies, 8250 participants) when using the most conservative effect estimates, indicating a slight improvement in practice with tailored strategies compared to no strategy (Analysis 2.1; Figure). As part of the sensitivity analysis using the least conservative reported result, the pooled odds ratio also indicated a slight improvement in professional practice with tailored strategies compared to no strategy (OR 1.51, 95% CI 1.28 to 1.79; P < 0.0001; Analysis 2.2, Supplementary material 7). This analysis excluded Betrán 2018, which reported a maximum OR of 1858 for the introduction of screening for proteinuria. Because this represents a new practice, it is expected that the OR will be inflated due to the low level of pre‐intervention practice. When we included the results from Betrán 2018, the pooled OR for tailored strategy versus no strategy was 2.16 (95% CI 1.19 to 3.93; P = 0.01; Analysis 2.6; Supplementary material 7).

Continuous outcomes

We could not include the remaining 32 studies in the meta‐analyses, so we included them in the vote‐counting analysis (see Table). Twenty‐eight studies showed an effect in the direction that supported the tailored strategy.

Nine of the 12 studies that compared tailored and non‐tailored strategies showed a direction of effect in favour of the tailored strategy (Althabe 2019, De Ruijter 2018, Etxeberria 2018, Foy 2004, LaBresh 2014; Langham 2002, Roos‐Blom 2019, Rosario 2022; Soumerai 1998).Nineteen of the 20 studies that compared tailored strategies to no strategy showed a direction of effect in favour of the tailored strategy (see Table).

These results from the vote‐counting analysis consider effects without regard to whether the outcome was statistically significant.

Important outcomes

We did not examine any secondary outcomes in this review.

Subgroup analysis

Types of effect measures

We performed formal subgroup analyses by effect measure type (OR, RR, rate ratio) and found no statistically significant differences between the type of effect measures used in either the comparison with the non‐tailored strategies (P = 0.64; Analysis 1.3; Supplementary material 7), or the comparison with no strategy (P = 0.31; Analysis 2.3; Supplementary material 7).

Inputs used in the tailoring process

A clear trend across the number of elements (1: Use of theory; 2: Evidence of strategy effectiveness; 3: Input from stakeholders) was not evident in tailored versus non‐tailored strategies. However, there was a significant subgroup difference in effects by the number of tailoring elements used (P < 0.0001). Subgroup ORs ranged from 1.03 (95% CI 0.89 to 1.20) for use of one element to 4.79 (95% CI 0.95 to 24.1) for use of three elements (Analysis 1.4; Figure).

Note: Effect estimates are labelled as odds ratios for consistency across the forest plot. However, some included studies originally reported other effect measures. In this analysis, French 2022, Frethaim 2006, and Zou 2016 reported a risk ratio [58, 116, 209], while Kakande 2022, Perez 2015, and van Uum 2020 reported a rate ratio [135, 146, 238]. All other studies in this plot reported odds ratios.

When comparing tailored strategies with no strategy, we did not see subgroup differences (P = 0.31), meaning that the number of inputs did not modify the finding that a tailored strategy improved outcomes compared to no strategy. Subgroup ORs ranged from 1.15 (95% CI 1.03 to 1.27) for use of two elements to 1.47 (95% CI 1.00 to 2.16) for use of one element (Analysis 2.4; Figure).

Tailored versus non‐tailored strategies

Three studies were performed in countries not classified as high‐income: Kakande 2022 (low‐income); Zou 2016 (upper middle‐income); and King 2021 (lower middle‐income). The test for subgroup differences was significant, as expected, given the strong significant results reported by Zou 2016 and null results reported by Kakande 2022 and King 2021 (df = 3, P = 0.0005). Amongst studies performed in high‐income countries, the pooled OR was 1.65 (95% CI 1.30 to 2.09; P < 0.0001, weight = 78%; Analysis 1.5; Figure), which was not materially different to the results including all studies.

Tailored strategies versus no strategy

Only two studies were performed in countries not classified as high‐income countries: Betrán 2018 (low‐income) and Huffman 2018 (lower middle‐income). Removing these two studies did not materially change the results (pooled OR 1.32, 95% CI 1.16 to 1.50; P < 0.0001; weight = 89%; Analysis 2.5; Figure). Although the test for subgroup analysis was significant, as expected, given the null findings in Huffman 2018 and strongly significant findings in Betrán 2018, caution is warranted in the interpretation of subgroup differences due to the small number of studies classified outside high income (df = 2, P < 0.0001).

Sensitivity analysis

We carried out sensitivity analyses defined in advance to assess the robustness of our conclusions and explore the impact on effect sizes, which involved restricting the analysis to studies at low risk of bias and using the least conservative effect measures. As described above, pooled estimates were not materially affected when using the least conservative estimates, in part because of the small number of studies with more than one estimate.

We assessed two studies at low risk of bias for the comparison of tailored versus non‐tailored strategies: Guthrie 2016 (OR 1.16, 95% CI 0.32 to 4.15) and Zou 2016 (OR 1.92, 95% CI 1.38 to 2.68). The pooled OR for these studies was 1.86 (95% CI 1.35 to 2.56; P = 0.0002), with Zou 2016 contributing 94% of the weighting.We assessed three studies at low risk of bias for the comparison of tailored strategies versus no strategy: Brown 2018 (OR 1.06, 95% CI 0.74 to 1.51); Garg 2024 (OR 1.00, 95% CI 0.87 to 1.15) and Scott 2013 (OR 1.37, 95% CI 0.97 to 1.94). The pooled OR for these studies was 1.07 (95% CI 0.91 to 1.27, P = 0.41), with Garg 2024 contributing 62% of the weighting, and the others approximately 19% each.

Equity assessment

As part of the sensitivity analysis, we were interested in the effect of tailored implementation strategies amongst high‐ and low‐ or middle‐income countries. Too few studies were performed outside high‐income countries to test for effect modification by World Bank economic classification (tailored versus non‐tailored strategies: Kakande 2022 (low‐income); Zou 2016 (upper middle‐income), and King 2021 (lower middle‐income); tailored versus no strategy: Betrán 2018 (low‐income) and Huffman 2018 (lower middle‐income)).

Reporting biases

We generated funnel plots for both comparisons to explore possible publication biases. While the funnel plot depicting the main comparison of the review (tailored strategies versus non‐tailored strategies) showed some slight asymmetry, this was not thought to be substantial enough to be of significant concern (Figure). The funnel plot depicting the tailored versus no strategy comparison showed a more symmetrical pattern, suggesting that there was no indication of publication bias (Figure). The risk of publication bias may be mitigated by the extensive search strategy used for this review update, including checking trials registers and ongoing studies.

Figure 9. Funnel plot of tailored intervention vs non‐tailored intervention

Figure 10. Funnel plot of tailored intervention vs no intervention

Discussion

Summary of main results

The aim of this review was to provide an updated assessment of the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies on the implementation of recommended clinical practice in healthcare settings, in comparison to a non‐tailored strategy or no strategy. The review synthesised evidence from 79 studies, involving 25,630 healthcare professionals (based on studies reporting participant numbers) in a wide variety of settings and health systems (Table). However, very few studies were carried out in low‐ and middle‐income settings. We identified 52 additional studies since the previous version of the review in 2015 [12]. This suggests a substantial increase in the number of studies examining the effectiveness of tailored strategies over the last 10 years. Overall, tailored implementation strategies, compared to non‐tailored strategies or no strategy, probably lead to a slight improvement in professional practice. Our confidence in the evidence, assessed by GRADE, is moderate due to substantial heterogeneity in the results shown in both visual inconsistency in forest plots and statistical measures of heterogeneity (I2 = 82%).

Limitations of the evidence included in the review

In this review update, we identified evidence from a substantial number of studies that assessed the effectiveness of tailored strategies in a range of healthcare settings using a variety of measures of professional practice. Findings largely aligned with and built on the original reviews due to the increased availability of empirical research. The gaps that still pervade the evidence base include a lack of detailed and consistent reporting of the tailoring process as well as evidence of effectiveness in low‐ and middle‐income countries.

As noted in the previous review, there was substantial heterogeneity in approaches and resulting strategies. Most tailored strategies were multifaceted with an average of five strategies used to improve professional practice. Applying the ERIC taxonomy to tailored strategies [17], the most used strategies included 'Distribute educational materials' (in the form of guidelines, copies of articles, summary documents, e‐learning packages), 'Audit and provide feedback', and 'Conduct educational meetings' (including online meetings), 'Educational outreach' (either on a one‐to‐one basis or with groups) and 'Reminders' used either in consultations (frequently generated by computer systems) or in mailings or meetings. The preponderance of educational strategies suggests that strategies may not be tailored to the most influential barriers. In this review there is no assessment of the appropriateness or strength of the match between determinants of practice and the selected strategy, a limitation discussed in more detail in the next section.

The format and mode of delivery of strategies varied across studies. It may be that the core components confer effectiveness regardless of whether the strategy is tailored or not, as discussed by the authors of the previous review. For example, audit and feedback was one of the most common strategies, and it has been shown to consistently improve professional practice [258]. The field has yet to offer practical and accessible theoretical guidance on how and why strategies work, therefore limiting the extent to which strategies can be selected based on their mechanisms to target barriers.

Numerous phases of data collection and a variety of methods were used as part of the tailoring process. Most studies used qualitative methods or surveys to identify determinants or select strategies, or both. We examined the extent to which studies drew explicitly on theories, models and frameworks, and evidence or input from stakeholders as part of the tailoring process. These elements are proposed as important ingredients in the tailoring process [9], as well as intervention development more generally [259]. Only 16 studies in the review drew on all three elements, perhaps reflective of the practical challenges and lack of evidence and guidance on optimal approaches, for example, on how to involve stakeholders in the tailoring process. It would also be valuable to explore which stakeholders were involved, how and at what stages in the tailoring process (identification of determinants versus selection of strategies). Ramanadhan and colleagues discuss the importance of participatory implementation approaches in understanding and aligning implementation determinants, implementation strategies, mechanisms, and outcomes [260]. The extent to which participatory methods were used may influence the match between determinants of practice and the strategy selected to address these factors. O’Cathain developed a taxonomy of approaches to developing interventions to improve health, specifying eight categories and associated actions (partnership; target population‐centred; evidence and theory‐based; implementation‐based; efficiency‐based; stepped or phased; intervention‐specific and a combination approach) [261]. Applying this taxonomy to descriptions of the tailoring process could allow for greater synthesis of approaches coupled with consistent reporting of the tailoring process, for example, using reporting guidelines for implementation strategies [262].

Only eight studies assessed the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies in low‐ and middle‐income countries. Only two of these studies were eligible for meta‐analysis, hence there is insufficient evidence to draw conclusions about effectiveness in these settings. Our conclusions about the characteristics and effectiveness of tailored strategies are based on health systems in high‐ and middle‐income countries, which likely face different implementation challenges and, as a result, may select different implementation strategies to improve professional practice. Determinants specific to implementation in low‐ and middle‐income countries have been identified [263]. A recent systematic review of the application of the ERIC compilation of implementation strategies in low‐and middle‐income countries identified 60 studies, one‐third of which were published in the final year of the review period (March 2022‐2023), suggesting that evidence is emerging [264]. In that review, most studies described constituent strategies that matched with the ERIC compilation. However, only one study evaluated implementation effectiveness.

Further limitations of the evidence in this review relate to incomplete reporting of participant numbers in included studies, different approaches to adjustment, and variation in effect measure. Across the 79 included studies, participant counts were unclear or not reported in 38 studies, including 10 of the studies that contributed to the meta‐analysis comparing tailored versus non‐tailored strategies. This did not affect the calculation of effect estimates, which were based on the available denominators for each study. We therefore judged that the missing participant counts did not alter our judgements of the certainty of the evidence, although they reduced transparency and limited confidence in describing the overall scale of the evidence base. Missing participant counts also limited our ability to provide an exact overall sample size, and introduced some uncertainty regarding the scale and representativeness of the evidence. In the meta‐analysis, we opted to pool relative findings whether odds, risk or rate ratios. The majority of studies reported odds ratios, but few papers added detail of the procedures and statistical options used, so we could not confirm the type of measure reported. Odds ratios are generally further from the null than risk ratios, converging when outcomes are rare or there is no effect of intervention, thus the decision not to transform risk ratios to odds ratios, while a limitation, is conservative. In addition, the heterogeneity in effect measures due to the intervention and outcome is likely to outweigh any heterogeneity due to effect measure differences.

We judged most included studies to be at low risk of bias or to have some concerns, reflecting both the generally robust study design in the included studies and the use of the RoB 2 tool. Unlike the original RoB tool, which often resulted in 'unclear' ratings where reporting was insufficient, RoB 2 requires review authors to make a domain‐level judgement (low, some concerns, high) guided by signalling questions. This framework reduces the frequency of 'unclear' ratings, which in earlier reviews could make the risk of bias profile appear worse than it might truly be. However, the existence of at least some concerns for all domains outlined below may still limit confidence in some findings.

In addition to the heterogeneity in design mentioned above, statistical heterogeneity was evident. The I2 statistics were in the range considered high for many of the random‐effects meta‐analyses, but consistent with EPOC reviews focused on interventions in diverse clinical areas with diverse outcome measures. To check for sources of heterogeneity, we performed three prespecified subgroup analyses. However, heterogeneity remained within most of the levels of the subgroups, therefore not explaining the overall high I2 statistics. A large I2 statistic might indicate that the studies should not be pooled due to the variation in their reported findings. First, we note that nearly all effect estimates were in the direction of a beneficial effect of the intervention, though not all statistically significant. Second, although relative measures of association will remove outcome units of measure and some heterogeneity inherent in using different scales and instruments to measure different outcomes, they remain influenced by the prevalence of the practice before the intervention (for example, the same intervention can have a relatively bigger impact when the baseline prevalence of the practice is perhaps 30%, compared to the impact possible when practice is already at, say, 75%). Third, we considered the limitations of the I2 statistic in that it represents the proportion of variance from differences in true effects (between studies) relative to total variance (between‐study variance plus random error (within studies)), and does not quantify the magnitude of the distribution of true effects included in the meta‐analyses. Some of the studies reported confidence intervals for the intervention effects that were narrow and did not overlap with others. This will simultaneously inflate the between‐study variance and deflate the within‐study variance. The differences in the magnitude of effects and confidence intervals contributing to mathematical heterogeneity can be explained in part by the range of populations, sample sizes, interventions, and outcomes, as well as effect measures and adjustment strategies. Nevertheless, we believe the data remain appropriate for meta‐analysis, and this synthesis approach represents an appropriate method to address the research question.

Certainty of evidence

We conducted a GRADE assessment for the main comparison of this review, tailored versus non‐tailored strategies. Tailored implementation strategies probably result in a slight improvement in professional practice, compared to non‐tailored strategies. Our confidence in the evidence is moderate. We downgraded our rating for the inconsistency domain by one due to the presence of visual inconsistency in the forest plot, in addition to the statistical analyses indicating a high level of heterogeneity. We did not downgrade the other domains.

Limitations of the review processes

This review supersedes the previous review published in 2015 [12]. In keeping with the previous review, we continued to focus on the implementation of clinical practice guidelines, one type of evidence‐based intervention. As such, the results may not be applicable to tailored implementation of other types of interventions. This is important considering the ongoing debate about the nature of the evidence and the definition of evidence‐based interventions and practices [265, 266].

Also, in line with the original review, we defined tailored strategies as collections of “strategies to improve professional practice that are planned, taking account of prospectively identified determinants of practice” with the stipulation that the identification of determinants must have taken place before the selection of the strategy. While some of the studies included in the review did use ‘tailor strategies’ as part of the change bundle (Cadilhac 2022; Cheater 2006; Lewis 2022; Llewelyn 2022; Perez 2015; Rodrigues 2019; Schouten 2007), we did not examine the effectiveness of strategies that were not tailored but that involved ongoing tailoring as a component (that is ongoing identification of determinants and responsive selection of strategies to address new barriers) or preselected strategies that often incorporate ongoing tailoring as part of a multifaceted approach. Such adaptive implementation strategies are often referred to as tailoring [267]. It's important to examine these approaches to ongoing tailoring and the appropriateness and impact of the resulting strategies. It's possible that implementation strategies such as practice facilitation, which enable ongoing consideration of and adjustment to local context, are most closely aligned with the conceptualisation of tailoring. It is expected that implementation interventions will change as implementation progresses in practice. For example, when tracking implementation strategies over the course of a four‐year, hybrid, type 2 effectiveness‐implementation study, Smith and colleagues found the small number of strategy changes were mostly unplanned and due to the changing healthcare context or to address emergent barriers [268]. Tools have been developed to systematically and longitudinally track implementation strategies [269, 270], including within tailored approaches to implementation in low‐ and middle‐income countries [271].

Amongst the 79 studies in the review, there were 378 outcome measures of professional practice (193 primary, 214 secondary). Professional practice was defined using different behaviours, such as prescribing, treatment or diagnostic behaviours. While we pooled a relatively wide variety of outcomes, all related to processes of care reflecting professional practice and were used to assess the effectiveness of a tailored strategy. We devised a series of decision rules to ensure consistency and transparency in the selection of measures of effect for inclusion in the meta‐analysis. Assessing the effectiveness of tailored implementation strategies based on changes in professional practice has limitations. We did not assess the effect of tailored strategies on other outcomes such as the attitudes, knowledge and perceptions of healthcare professionals, which may reflect important mechanisms of change. For example, a recent study by Vis compared a self‐guided tailoring toolkit for local implementers compared to implementation as usual and found a positive effect on the normalisation of delivery of internet‐based cognitive behavioural therapy [272].

Unlike the previous review update [12], we did not include patient outcomes in the assessment. Instead, we focused on measures of professional practice, which as a proximal outcome is a better indication of whether the intervention was effective or not. Tailored implementation strategies typically target those responsible for managing, co‐ordinating or delivering the evidence‐based intervention, rather than those in receipt of the intervention. However, some studies of tailored implementation strategies did target patients and service users. We plan to further interrogate these studies in future analyses, including examining the role of patients and service users in the tailoring process.

Overall, 35 studies compared tailored implementation strategies to non‐tailored strategies. We coded the strategies in the intervention arm using the ERIC compilation [17], but did not code strategies in the non‐tailored or control arms. Future research should examine the number, type and similarity of strategies used in the control arm. This would require detailed and consistent reporting of control strategies. It could illuminate the extent to which tailoring results in radically or marginally different strategies and where the difference lies. This type of analysis may also suggest whether the mechanisms that drive the effectiveness of tailoring are related to things like increasing the alignment between strategies and determinants, increasing consensus and buy‐in amongst implementers who inform the tailoring process, or both [273]. Examining the type of strategies included in the control arm could also tell us more about implementation as usual, an under‐researched topic [274, 275]. Implementation as usual may suggest the types of strategies that are acceptable, feasible, and affordable for health services and where tailoring or adaptation is most needed.

Twenty included studies used the strategy ‘Providing patient education materials’ as part of their tailored implementation activities, targeting patients or consumers alongside healthcare professionals. We developed this strategy label based on coding of strategy descriptions; it is not a formal label within the ERIC taxonomy [17]. The ERIC strategies targeting patients and consumers are broadly defined. It could be argued that providing patient education materials fits within the strategy ‘Prepare patients/consumers to be active participants’. However, we considered the coding to be a more accurate reflection of the implementation activity.

It could be argued that tailored interventions should not be evaluated and synthesised as traditional interventions, given that tailoring is a process that, by design, leads to heterogeneous interventions. A realist review approach may be more beneficial for understanding how tailoring works, for whom, in what circumstances and why – reflecting that tailoring can be researcher‐ or implementer‐led and the approaches to tailoring are context‐ and resource‐dependent. As mentioned above, we did not assess the level at which implementation strategies were tailored (e.g. tailoring strategies to an organisation, clinic or unit, professional group) or the quality of tailoring (e.g. appropriateness or strength of the match between determinants of practice and the constituent strategies). As such, we cannot draw conclusions about fidelity to concept or quality of tailoring. However, we believe this is an important future direction for understanding tailoring. We are conducting an ongoing scoping review to map the conceptualisation and approaches to tailoring and plan to examine this more closely as part of further supplementary analysis of the studies in this review [276].

We worked with an information specialist (NR) to undertake a comprehensive search strategy across multiple databases, including three trials registers, aligned with Cochrane guidance. The most recent search was conducted in March 2024. There were a few changes to the research team over the course of this review; however, we devised the protocol before the review process commenced and adhered to Cochrane standards throughout. During screening and data extraction, we held regular team meetings, and completed review steps in duplicate, involving a third review author to resolve conflicts. It was challenging to operationalise some aspects of the data extraction and coding due to the level of description in individual papers and across papers relating to a particular study. For example, it was difficult to ascertain with certainty whether and how theories, models or frameworks were used in the tailoring process or whether the mechanisms of action were described. To maximise consistency, we piloted processes and refined them at each stage. We provided concrete examples to ensure that each team member was following the correct procedure and that the tools, such as the data extraction spreadsheet, were fit for purpose.

A strength of our review is the use of vote counting based on the direction of the effect, in line with Cochrane best practice recommendations. This method avoids over‐reliance on statistical significance, which can be problematic when studies are underpowered or report only P values. By focusing on the consistency and direction of effects, we provide a more meaningful interpretation of the overall evidence when a formal meta‐analysis is not feasible.

Finally, the search for this update was conducted up to March 2024, a potential limitation of the review. Since finalising the synthesis of included studies, we identified 29 ongoing studies and 34 potentially eligible completed studies (according to their trials register) which are awaiting classification. Of those awaiting classification, seven studies had a peer‐reviewed results paper, and two studies were available as preprints at the time of writing. Due to resource constraints, we were not in a position to integrate these studies into the review. However, having examined their results, we do not expect these studies to change the overall results or conclusions of the current review. Our conclusions are consistent with the original review and previous updates [10, 11, 12]. The number of studies ongoing and awaiting classification highlights the burgeoning body of research evaluating the effect of tailored strategies and calls into question the feasibility of staying on top of this rapidly emerging literature. The topic may warrant a living systematic review to incorporate relevant new evidence as it emerges.

Agreements and disagreements with other studies or reviews

Most of the systematic reviews of tailoring focus on tailoring the clinical or behavioural interventions rather than the interventions supporting implementation [277, 278]. As in 2015, the only reviews that directly investigate the effect of tailored implementation strategies are previous versions of this review [10, 11, 12]. In the 2015 version by Baker and colleagues, 15 studies compared a tailored strategy to a non‐tailored strategy [12]. Like our results, tailored strategies were likely to be slightly more effective than non‐tailored strategies.

A recent systematic review of 129 studies of experimentally tested implementation strategies published between 2010 and 2022 identified tailoring as one of the most used strategies across health and human services [279]. Tailoring as a strategy was tested in 41 studies and associated with positive results 67% of the time. Tailoring was typically paired with distribution of educational materials, education meetings, audit and feedback and external facilitation. Facilitation is a natural complement to tailoring as it involves multiple implementation‐oriented and support‐oriented roles. Implementation‐oriented roles can include adapting project requirements to local context and helping to identify and determine solutions to address potential barriers to project or change implementation [280]. Our findings mirror this review in terms of the most used implementation strategies. We also identified 'Distribute educational materials', 'Audit and provide feedback', and 'Conduct educational meetings' as the most common implementation strategies.

Authors' conclusions

Implications for practice

Tailored implementation strategies probably lead to a slight improvement in professional practice compared to non‐tailored strategies (moderate‐certainty evidence), and results indicate that tailored strategies lead to a slight improvement in professional practice compared to no strategy. It is not clear how or why tailored strategies drive changes in practice more effectively than non‐tailored approaches.

Equity‐related implications for practice

The evidence in this review is based largely on studies conducted in high‐ and middle‐income countries. There is insufficient evidence to draw conclusions about the effect of tailored implementation interventions in low‐ and middle‐income countries. As formal implementation research expands in these settings, there will be greater opportunities to consider the equity‐related implications of the tailoring process and the resulting tailored intervention. There are resource implications to conducting tailoring as the number of steps, types of stakeholders, and choice of methods are likely influenced by resources available for tailoring in a setting.

Implications for research

A substantial number of studies evaluated the effectiveness of tailored strategies on professional practice. However, questions remain about the quality of tailoring and what makes tailored strategies more effective. Studies need to address the following areas.

The quality of tailoring or fidelity to the concept, such as exploring the appropriateness or strength of the match between determinants of practice and the selected strategy. This could include examining the level at which implementation strategies are tailored and the extent to which and how they differ from non‐tailored strategies.Approaches and methods used for tailoring; the variety of methods, levels, and varied inputs into the tailoring process (theory, evidence, input from key stakeholders) means there is no single ‘best’ approach to tailoring. Future research should consider approaches and methods used for tailoring, using existing taxonomies of intervention or strategy development processes to allow for greater synthesis across studies.Consistent and detailed reporting of the tailoring process, using or adapting existing reporting guidelines [262].Costing and cost‐effectiveness, to understand the return on investment in tailoring methodologies, particularly for low‐resource settings where implementation of evidence‐based interventions may be most needed.

The quality of tailoring or fidelity to the concept, such as exploring the appropriateness or strength of the match between determinants of practice and the selected strategy. This could include examining the level at which implementation strategies are tailored and the extent to which and how they differ from non‐tailored strategies.
